If the umpire made a call he didn't like, he'd throw his glove in the dirt. Real dramatic type. \n\nI never understood getting that worked up over a game. I played right field, mostly. It's peaceful out there. You get a lot of time to just watch the grass grow. The city maintained the fields, and they always cut the grass way too short in August, so it would burn up and get all yellow and crunchy. You had to wear decent cleats or you'd slip right on the dead grass. I had a pair of Nike cleats, black with a white swoosh. Bought them at a sporting goods store that used to be over by the old mall. They tore that mall down a few years ago to build luxury condos. They look completely out of place, all glass and steel right in the middle of a brick neighborhood. \n\nBut anyway, no, my mood is fine. I'm just tired of the constant auditing and the paper trail they're making. That's the only thing bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about these audits caused you to miss work, withdraw from social activities, or change your daily routine in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't missed any work. I mean, you can't miss work when they're watching your every move, right? But I have had to change how I do things. Like, I started avoiding certain network projects completely because I know they're just setting traps in the configurations. And I spend half my day writing these massive, detailed notes in every single ticket just to cover myself. It's exhausting. \n\nI used to use this really nice mechanical keyboard for typing all those notes—a Cherry MX Brown, tactile but not too clicky, you know? The IT guys used to let us bring our own peripherals before they locked down the USB ports. Now we're stuck with these mushy membrane keyboards that Dell ships for free with the workstations. They feel like typing on wet oatmeal. My wrists ache by the end of the day. I tried bringing in one of those ergonomic gel wrist rests, the ones that look like a blue sausage, but someone stole it off my desk over the weekend. Probably the night cleaning crew. They always leave the trash cans slightly out of place, too. It drives me crazy. I have to push mine exactly two inches from the desk leg every morning. \n\nBut yeah, because I have to write all these defensive notes, it's really slowed down my work, and the team meetings have gotten incredibly tense. Nobody wants to collaborate anymore because I keep pointing out the flaws in their ticket routing. So I guess you could say my routine has changed, but only because I have to protect myself from the audits.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped seeing friends or family, or stopped participating in hobbies you used to enjoy, specifically because you are concerned that your coworkers might be tracking your activities through those interactions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think they're tracking my family. Outside of this whole mess with the ticket rotation and the audits at work, everything else is fine. I still keep my house in order, you know? I pay the bills on time, I help my wife out with whatever she needs. \n\nActually, I still write a physical check for our water bill because the city's online portal charges a $2.50 convenience fee. It's ridiculous. Two dollars and fifty cents just to process an automated clearing house transfer. It's a complete racket. The city council voted on that last November, and my neighbor, Tom, he actually went down to the town hall meeting to complain about it. Tom’s got this old basset hound that barks at the mail carrier every single day at exactly 1:15 PM. You can practically set your watch by that dog. The mail carrier drives one of those old right-hand drive Grumman LLVs, you know, the aluminum ones? They're notorious for catching fire because the windshield wiper fluid line can leak right onto the fuse box. I read a whole article about it in a mechanics magazine a few years ago. They were supposed to replace that entire fleet by 2018, but you know how government contracts work. Total bureaucratic nightmare. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my family. No, I still see my friends and family. My personal life hasn't changed. I do my chores, and honestly, I still handle all the technical parts of my job perfectly well, too. Nobody is going around saying my actual work is sloppy or disorganized. I know my stuff. Unlike the new guy they hired in Tier 1 support, Kevin. He leaves ethernet cables tangled up like a bowl of spaghetti behind the server racks. I had to spend an hour last Tuesday just untangling a Cat6 run because he didn't bother to use the velcro ties. \n\nSo, no, I haven't stopped my hobbies or anything. It's just the audits at work that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever asked a trusted coworker, someone not involved in management, if they have noticed these same security audits and ticket patterns being used against them?\n\nPatient: Ask a coworker? No, I haven't really asked anyone else about it. I mean, who can you trust, right? Even the guys who aren't in management, they're all just trying to keep their heads down and get through the day. It's like that time I tried to organize a carpool back in 2005. Gas prices were spiking—I think it was right after Hurricane Katrina hit the refineries down in the Gulf. We were paying something like three dollars a gallon, which seemed insane back then. I drove a Honda Accord, the '98 model with the V6 engine. Great car, incredibly reliable, but the transmission on those things was a known issue. You had to make sure you changed the fluid every 30,000 miles or the clutch packs would wear out prematurely. I always used the genuine Honda ATF-Z1 fluid. You could buy it by the case online for a decent discount. \n\nAnyway, I asked three guys on my shift if they wanted to split the driving, and every single one of them had some excuse. \"Oh, I have to pick up my kids,\" or \"I go to the gym after work.\" Nobody wants to stick their neck out or commit to anything. People are just inherently selfish, you know? They want the convenience without the responsibility. Like how people just leave their grocery carts in the middle of the parking lot instead of walking ten feet to the corral. The wind catches them and they slam right into your door. I got a nasty dent in my quarter panel last year at the Safeway because of that. The paint actually chipped down to the primer. \n\nSo, no, I haven't brought up the audits with anyone else. I just keep it to myself and try to stay one step ahead of the tickets.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretation of these work audits might be influenced by a health condition, rather than being an objective reality?\n\nPatient: A health condition? Like I'm making it up? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, I'm here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that this new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me these logs, pointing to the screen and saying, \"Look, it’s just policy, everyone goes through it.\" But it's not just policy. \n\nIt reminds me of when they changed the policy on the break room refrigerator back in '98. They put up this laminated sign saying anything left on Friday at 5 PM would be thrown out. \"Just policy,\" they said. I used to bring in these amazing homemade pastrami sandwiches. My wife would get the meat from this little deli downtown, Shapiro's. They slow-roasted it, sliced it paper-thin. You can't find pastrami like that anymore. Now it's all pre-packaged, water-injected garbage at the supermarket. The sodium content alone is enough to give you a stroke. I read a study once about how nitrates in deli meat are linked to all sorts of digestive issues. \n\nAnyway, I left half a sandwich in there one Friday because I got pulled into a massive server crash right at 4:45. By the time I got back on Monday, the sandwich was gone, but my Tupperware container was sitting clean on the drying rack. If they threw it out, why did they wash the container? Someone ate it, I know they did. Probably Dave from accounting. He always lingered around the fridge. \n\nSo when management tells me these audits are just policy, I know better. I know they're targeting me, trying to build a case to push me out. It has nothing to do with my health or my mind playing tricks on me. The proof is right there in the ticket assignments, even if they won't admit it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I feel like we already covered some of this, honestly. Like I said a few minutes ago, outside of this whole issue with the ticket rotation and the audits, everything else is completely fine. I still keep my house in order, I pay the bills, I help my wife out. 

Speaking of keeping the house in order, I actually just had to rebuild the brush roller on our vacuum cleaner this past weekend. It's an old Hoover WindTunnel. They don't make them like that anymore, you know. The new ones, they use these cheap plastic impellers that snap if you accidentally suck up a single penny. The older models have metal impellers. Built like a tank. Anyway, the drive belt snapped. You can't just buy them at the local hardware store anymore, you have to order them online. I had to wait four days for a two-dollar rubber belt to ship all the way from Ohio. Ohio! Why is a warehouse in Ohio handling distribution for a single residential address when there's a major fulfillment center two towns over? It makes absolutely no sense. The logistics algorithms these shipping companies use are completely backwards. They route packages through three different regional hubs just to save a fraction of a cent on bulk freight, but it ends up taking twice as long for the consumer. 

But, uh, what were you asking? Oh, hygiene and getting to work. No, no problems there. I'm up at 5:30 every morning. Shower, shave. I actually use an old-school double-edge safety razor. Those five-blade cartridge things you see at the drugstore are a total scam. They just keep adding blades to justify charging twenty bucks a pack. It's basic marketing psychology. 

So, yeah, I'm at my desk at 7:45 AM sharp every single day. I handle all the technical parts of my job perfectly. Nobody is going around saying my work is sloppy or disorganized, because it isn't. I do my job well. It's just those audits that are the problem, nothing else.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's a pleasure to meet you; could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. Smith, I’m here because... honestly, for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone, to actually take me seriously. It’s about work. They instituted this new ticket rotation and these sudden security audits, and it’s obvious they’re being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pulling me in, showing me these server logs and insisting it’s just company policy. \n\nWhich is ridiculous, you know, because back in the late 90s when we used the old Remedy ticketing system—I don't know if you're familiar with Remedy, but it was all green screens and clunky interfaces. My manager back then, Dave, he loved it. Dave used to bring in a dozen donuts every Friday. Always the maple bars. I can’t eat maple bars anymore, my stomach just can't handle the glaze at my age. But anyway, Dave never needed to audit my tickets. Now, suddenly, every single thing I touch is being tracked for this supposed \"policy.\"\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing any sounds or voices that other people seem unable to hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, nothing like that. My hearing is perfectly fine. Well, mostly fine, I guess. I did have to get it checked a few years ago because I kept missing the microwave beep at home. Turns out it was just a buildup of wax. The clinic doctor used this little water spray thing to flush it out—it was freezing cold, honestly, you'd think they could warm the water up first. \n\nIt actually reminded me of the time I had to deal with a leak in the server room at my old job back in '04. The AC unit was dripping right onto the main switch, just this steady drip, drip, drip. Took the facilities guys three days to get out there, and I had to rig up a plastic tarp over the whole rack just to keep the network online. They never even thanked me for that, if you can believe it. But no, no voices or secret sounds or anything. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these specific actions by your current management are part of a targeted, organized effort to sabotage your career, or do you feel that most people in your life—including friends and family—are generally untrustworthy and likely to take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s definitely the management at work. My family, they're fine. My sister calls every Sunday, though honestly, she talks my ear off about her golden retriever, Buster. Buster this, Buster that. He chewed up her good rug last week, the Persian one she got at an estate sale. I told her she should have crate-trained him when he was a puppy, but she never listens. She’s always been like that, even when we were kids. \n\nBut at work? Oh, absolutely. It's a coordinated effort. It’s not just one person, it’s the whole chain of command. The HR director, the IT manager... they're all in on it. They know I’m close to retirement, and they want to push me out before my pension fully vests. It’s exactly what happened to Frank over in accounting back in 2012. They started nitpicking his expense reports, making him justify every single paperclip. Frank used to buy those fancy colored paperclips, the plastic-coated ones? I never saw the point of those, they slide right off the paper if you stack more than five sheets. But yeah, they drove Frank right out the door, and now they’re doing the exact same thing to me with these ticket audits. It’s a completely targeted campaign.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people at your work have access to special technology, such as reading your thoughts or monitoring you through devices in your home, to keep track of your activities?\n\nPatient: Read my thoughts? No, no, they don't have anything like that. They don't need mind-reading technology, they just use the server logs and the ticket system. It's all right there on the network. They monitor my work computer, sure, but everyone knows they do that. It's in the employee handbook. I remember when they first updated the handbook back in... oh, it must have been 2008. They handed out these thick binders to everyone. Navy blue with the company logo stamped in silver on the front. \n\nI used that binder to prop up my monitor for years. You know how those old Dell monitors were, the stands were never quite the right height for my neck. I ended up having to go to a chiropractor for a while because of it. Dr. Evans, his name was. Nice guy, always had jazz playing in the waiting room. I don't really care for jazz, it's too unpredictable. I prefer something with a steady beat, like classic rock. Anyway, no, they aren't reading my mind or putting cameras in my house. They're just using the standard IT audits to build their case against me at the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that ordinary events, like a news report, a song on the radio, or strangers talking in public, are specifically intended to send you a secret message or are referring to you personally?\n\nPatient: Secret messages? On the radio? No, definitely not. I mean, the news is just the news, right? Mostly depressing these days, to be honest. I stopped watching the evening broadcast after they changed the anchor. The old guy, Tom, he had this really reassuring voice. Reminded me of my high school shop teacher, Mr. Henderson. \n\nMr. Henderson taught me how to use a lathe. We made these wooden bowls, and I gave mine to my mother for Mother's Day back in '81. She kept potpourri in it on the coffee table for years. It was that really strong cinnamon scent—almost gave you a headache if you sat too close to it. I always preferred pine, myself. Like those little tree air fresheners you hang in the car. I had one in my first car, a '78 Honda Civic. Great gas mileage on that thing, but the heater was absolute garbage in the winter. I had to scrape the ice off the *inside* of the windshield a few times. \n\nBut no, I don't think strangers or the radio are trying to send me messages. The only people out to get me are the ones at the office, using the ticket system. That's all I'm worried about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever asked your manager or HR directly about these audits, and if so, how did you interpret their explanation that it is just company policy?\n\nPatient: Oh, I’ve asked them. I mean, that's really why I'm here, because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone—anyone—to take this seriously. I marched right into the HR office. They moved HR to the third floor last spring and gave them this \"open concept\" layout. I absolutely hate it. You can hear everyone chewing their lunch. Sandra in payroll eats baby carrots at 11 AM every single day, and the crunching just echoes off the glass walls. I really don't know why they got rid of the old cubicles. The gray fabric walls absorbed the sound so much better. Plus, you could pin things to them. I used to have this Dilbert calendar pinned up, the one where you tear off a page every day? My daughter got it for me for Christmas. \n\nAnyway, yes, I confronted management about the new ticket rotation and these security audits. I told them point-blank that I know they're using them to build a paper trail against me. And you know what they do? They just pull up these logs on their screens, or they print them out to show me. And they print them on that cheap recycled paper that constantly jams the Lexmark printers on floor two. I’m always the one who has to go unjam those things. The rubber rollers get coated in paper dust because purchasing buys the bargain reams to save a few pennies. You really have to use a lint-free cloth with a little isopropyl alcohol to clean those rollers properly. If you just blow on it with canned air, it doesn't do any good, it just moves the dust around. \n\nBut yeah, they sit there, showing me these printed logs, and they just keep repeating the exact same corporate line: \"It's just company policy, we do this for everyone.\" They think I'm stupid. But I know it's not just policy. They're just using that as an excuse while they build their paper trail to get rid of me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the stress you feel regarding your job, have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently down or, conversely, having periods where you feel unusually energetic or irritable?\n\nPatient: Mood changes? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, obviously I'm stressed about the situation at work, who wouldn't be? But I'm not depressed or bouncing off the walls or anything. I just want to do my job. It's like when I used to play softball in the company league back in the late nineties. We had this pitcher, Dave, who worked in accounting. Dave was always so high-strung, you know? If the umpire made a call he didn't like, he'd throw his glove in the dirt. Real dramatic type. \n\nI never understood getting that worked up over a game. I played right field, mostly. It's peaceful out there. You get a lot of time to just watch the grass grow. The city maintained the fields, and they always cut the grass way too short in August, so it would burn up and get all yellow and crunchy. You had to wear decent cleats or you'd slip right on the dead grass. I had a pair of Nike cleats, black with a white swoosh. Bought them at a sporting goods store that used to be over by the old mall. They tore that mall down a few years ago to build luxury condos. They look completely out of place, all glass and steel right in the middle of a brick neighborhood. \n\nBut anyway, no, my mood is fine. I'm just tired of the constant auditing and the paper trail they're making. That's the only thing bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about these audits caused you to miss work, withdraw from social activities, or change your daily routine in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't missed any work. I mean, you can't miss work when they're watching your every move, right? But I have had to change how I do things. Like, I started avoiding certain network projects completely because I know they're just setting traps in the configurations. And I spend half my day writing these massive, detailed notes in every single ticket just to cover myself. It's exhausting. \n\nI used to use this really nice mechanical keyboard for typing all those notes—a Cherry MX Brown, tactile but not too clicky, you know? The IT guys used to let us bring our own peripherals before they locked down the USB ports. Now we're stuck with these mushy membrane keyboards that Dell ships for free with the workstations. They feel like typing on wet oatmeal. My wrists ache by the end of the day. I tried bringing in one of those ergonomic gel wrist rests, the ones that look like a blue sausage, but someone stole it off my desk over the weekend. Probably the night cleaning crew. They always leave the trash cans slightly out of place, too. It drives me crazy. I have to push mine exactly two inches from the desk leg every morning. \n\nBut yeah, because I have to write all these defensive notes, it's really slowed down my work, and the team meetings have gotten incredibly tense. Nobody wants to collaborate anymore because I keep pointing out the flaws in their ticket routing. So I guess you could say my routine has changed, but only because I have to protect myself from the audits.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped seeing friends or family, or stopped participating in hobbies you used to enjoy, specifically because you are concerned that your coworkers might be tracking your activities through those interactions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think they're tracking my family. Outside of this whole mess with the ticket rotation and the audits at work, everything else is fine. I still keep my house in order, you know? I pay the bills on time, I help my wife out with whatever she needs. \n\nActually, I still write a physical check for our water bill because the city's online portal charges a $2.50 convenience fee. It's ridiculous. Two dollars and fifty cents just to process an automated clearing house transfer. It's a complete racket. The city council voted on that last November, and my neighbor, Tom, he actually went down to the town hall meeting to complain about it. Tom’s got this old basset hound that barks at the mail carrier every single day at exactly 1:15 PM. You can practically set your watch by that dog. The mail carrier drives one of those old right-hand drive Grumman LLVs, you know, the aluminum ones? They're notorious for catching fire because the windshield wiper fluid line can leak right onto the fuse box. I read a whole article about it in a mechanics magazine a few years ago. They were supposed to replace that entire fleet by 2018, but you know how government contracts work. Total bureaucratic nightmare. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my family. No, I still see my friends and family. My personal life hasn't changed. I do my chores, and honestly, I still handle all the technical parts of my job perfectly well, too. Nobody is going around saying my actual work is sloppy or disorganized. I know my stuff. Unlike the new guy they hired in Tier 1 support, Kevin. He leaves ethernet cables tangled up like a bowl of spaghetti behind the server racks. I had to spend an hour last Tuesday just untangling a Cat6 run because he didn't bother to use the velcro ties. \n\nSo, no, I haven't stopped my hobbies or anything. It's just the audits at work that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever asked a trusted coworker, someone not involved in management, if they have noticed these same security audits and ticket patterns being used against them?\n\nPatient: Ask a coworker? No, I haven't really asked anyone else about it. I mean, who can you trust, right? Even the guys who aren't in management, they're all just trying to keep their heads down and get through the day. It's like that time I tried to organize a carpool back in 2005. Gas prices were spiking—I think it was right after Hurricane Katrina hit the refineries down in the Gulf. We were paying something like three dollars a gallon, which seemed insane back then. I drove a Honda Accord, the '98 model with the V6 engine. Great car, incredibly reliable, but the transmission on those things was a known issue. You had to make sure you changed the fluid every 30,000 miles or the clutch packs would wear out prematurely. I always used the genuine Honda ATF-Z1 fluid. You could buy it by the case online for a decent discount. \n\nAnyway, I asked three guys on my shift if they wanted to split the driving, and every single one of them had some excuse. \"Oh, I have to pick up my kids,\" or \"I go to the gym after work.\" Nobody wants to stick their neck out or commit to anything. People are just inherently selfish, you know? They want the convenience without the responsibility. Like how people just leave their grocery carts in the middle of the parking lot instead of walking ten feet to the corral. The wind catches them and they slam right into your door. I got a nasty dent in my quarter panel last year at the Safeway because of that. The paint actually chipped down to the primer. \n\nSo, no, I haven't brought up the audits with anyone else. I just keep it to myself and try to stay one step ahead of the tickets.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretation of these work audits might be influenced by a health condition, rather than being an objective reality?\n\nPatient: A health condition? Like I'm making it up? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, I'm here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that this new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me these logs, pointing to the screen and saying, \"Look, it’s just policy, everyone goes through it.\" But it's not just policy. \n\nIt reminds me of when they changed the policy on the break room refrigerator back in '98. They put up this laminated sign saying anything left on Friday at 5 PM would be thrown out. \"Just policy,\" they said. I used to bring in these amazing homemade pastrami sandwiches. My wife would get the meat from this little deli downtown, Shapiro's. They slow-roasted it, sliced it paper-thin. You can't find pastrami like that anymore. Now it's all pre-packaged, water-injected garbage at the supermarket. The sodium content alone is enough to give you a stroke. I read a study once about how nitrates in deli meat are linked to all sorts of digestive issues. \n\nAnyway, I left half a sandwich in there one Friday because I got pulled into a massive server crash right at 4:45. By the time I got back on Monday, the sandwich was gone, but my Tupperware container was sitting clean on the drying rack. If they threw it out, why did they wash the container? Someone ate it, I know they did. Probably Dave from accounting. He always lingered around the fridge. \n\nSo when management tells me these audits are just policy, I know better. I know they're targeting me, trying to build a case to push me out. It has nothing to do with my health or my mind playing tricks on me. The proof is right there in the ticket assignments, even if they won't admit it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, has your concern about these audits made it difficult for you to keep up with your personal hygiene, your household chores, or your ability to get to work on time?\n\nPatient: I feel like we already covered some of this, honestly. Like I said a few minutes ago, outside of this whole issue with the ticket rotation and the audits, everything else is completely fine. I still keep my house in order, I pay the bills, I help my wife out. \n\nSpeaking of keeping the house in order, I actually just had to rebuild the brush roller on our vacuum cleaner this past weekend. It's an old Hoover WindTunnel. They don't make them like that anymore, you know. The new ones, they use these cheap plastic impellers that snap if you accidentally suck up a single penny. The older models have metal impellers. Built like a tank. Anyway, the drive belt snapped. You can't just buy them at the local hardware store anymore, you have to order them online. I had to wait four days for a two-dollar rubber belt to ship all the way from Ohio. Ohio! Why is a warehouse in Ohio handling distribution for a single residential address when there's a major fulfillment center two towns over? It makes absolutely no sense. The logistics algorithms these shipping companies use are completely backwards. They route packages through three different regional hubs just to save a fraction of a cent on bulk freight, but it ends up taking twice as long for the consumer. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, hygiene and getting to work. No, no problems there. I'm up at 5:30 every morning. Shower, shave. I actually use an old-school double-edge safety razor. Those five-blade cartridge things you see at the drugstore are a total scam. They just keep adding blades to justify charging twenty bucks a pack. It's basic marketing psychology. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm at my desk at 7:45 AM sharp every single day. I handle all the technical parts of my job perfectly. Nobody is going around saying my work is sloppy or disorganized, because it isn't. I do my job well. It's just those audits that are the problem, nothing else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

No, I haven't really asked anyone else about it. I mean, who can you trust, right? Even the guys who aren't in management, they're all just trying to keep their heads down and get through the day. It's like that time I tried to organize a carpool back in 2005. Gas prices were spiking—I think it was right after Hurricane Katrina hit the refineries down in the Gulf. We were paying something like three dollars a gallon, which seemed insane back then. I drove a Honda Accord, the '98 model with the V6 engine. Great car, incredibly reliable, but the transmission on those things was a known issue. You had to make sure you changed the fluid every 30,000 miles or the clutch packs would wear out prematurely. I always used the genuine Honda ATF-Z1 fluid. You could buy it by the case online for a decent discount. \n\nAnyway, I asked three guys on my shift if they wanted to split the driving, and every single one of them had some excuse. \"Oh, I have to pick up my kids,\" or \"I go to the gym after work.\" Nobody wants to stick their neck out or commit to anything. People are just inherently selfish, you know? They want the convenience without the responsibility. Like how people just leave their grocery carts in the middle of the parking lot instead of walking ten feet to the corral. The wind catches them and they slam right into your door. I got a nasty dent in my quarter panel last year at the Safeway because of that. The paint actually chipped down to the primer. \n\nSo, no, I haven't brought up the audits with anyone else. I just keep it to myself and try to stay one step ahead of the tickets.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretation of these work audits might be influenced by a health condition, rather than being an objective reality?\n\nPatient: A health condition? Like I'm making it up? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, I'm here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that this new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me these logs, pointing to the screen and saying, \"Look, it’s just policy, everyone goes through it.\" But it's not just policy. \n\nIt reminds me of when they changed the policy on the break room refrigerator back in '98. They put up this laminated sign saying anything left on Friday at 5 PM would be thrown out. \"Just policy,\" they said. I used to bring in these amazing homemade pastrami sandwiches. My wife would get the meat from this little deli downtown, Shapiro's. They slow-roasted it, sliced it paper-thin. You can't find pastrami like that anymore. Now it's all pre-packaged, water-injected garbage at the supermarket. The sodium content alone is enough to give you a stroke. I read a study once about how nitrates in deli meat are linked to all sorts of digestive issues. \n\nAnyway, I left half a sandwich in there one Friday because I got pulled into a massive server crash right at 4:45. By the time I got back on Monday, the sandwich was gone, but my Tupperware container was sitting clean on the drying rack. If they threw it out, why did they wash the container? Someone ate it, I know they did. Probably Dave from accounting. He always lingered around the fridge. \n\nSo when management tells me these audits are just policy, I know better. I know they're targeting me, trying to build a case to push me out. It has nothing to do with my health or my mind playing tricks on me. The proof is right there in the ticket assignments, even if they won't admit it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, has your concern about these audits made it difficult for you to keep up with your personal hygiene, your household chores, or your ability to get to work on time?\n\nPatient: I feel like we already covered some of this, honestly. I still keep my house in order, I pay the bills, I help my wife out. \n\nSpeaking of keeping the house in order, I actually just had to rebuild the brush roller on our vacuum cleaner this past weekend. It's an old Hoover WindTunnel. They don't make them like that anymore, you know. The new ones, they use these cheap plastic impellers that snap if you accidentally suck up a single penny. The older models have metal impellers. Built like a tank. Anyway, the drive belt snapped. You can't just buy them at the local hardware store anymore, you have to order them online. I had to wait four days for a two-dollar rubber belt to ship all the way from Ohio. Ohio! Why is a warehouse in Ohio handling distribution for a single residential address when there's a major fulfillment center two towns over? It makes absolutely no sense. The logistics algorithms these shipping companies use are completely backwards. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Special talents? No, I'm just a computer support specialist. I don't have any kind of secret clearance or some grand insight that nobody else has. I mean, I'm good at my job, sure. I know my way around a network topology better than most of the kids they hire straight out of college these days. They come in with their degrees and their certifications, but they don't know how to troubleshoot a legacy system. 

It's like when they rolled out Windows Vista. What an absolute disaster that was. The User Account Control feature was so aggressive, it popped up a warning dialogue if you so much as breathed on the mouse. We spent six months just trying to get our proprietary inventory software to communicate with the new OS architecture. The software was written in Delphi, if you can believe it. Delphi! Nobody uses that anymore. It was developed by Borland back in the mid-90s. Actually, Borland had a really interesting corporate history. They were huge competitors with Microsoft for a while, especially with their Turbo Pascal compiler. Anders Hejlsberg, the guy who designed it, eventually got poached by Microsoft and ended up creating C#. The tech industry is incredibly incestuous like that. Everyone just moves from one giant corporation to another, taking the trade secrets with them. 

But no, I'm not some special genius or anything. I'm just a guy trying to do his job while management uses these audits to build a case against me. I don't have any delusions of grandeur, if that's what you're asking. I'm just a regular employee.

It's a pleasure to meet you; could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. Smith, I’m here because... honestly, for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone, to actually take me seriously. It’s about work. They instituted this new ticket rotation and these sudden security audits, and it’s obvious they’re being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pulling me in, showing me these server logs and insisting it’s just company policy. \n\nWhich is ridiculous, you know, because back in the late 90s when we used the old Remedy ticketing system—I don't know if you're familiar with Remedy, but it was all green screens and clunky interfaces. My manager back then, Dave, he loved it. Dave used to bring in a dozen donuts every Friday. Always the maple bars. I can’t eat maple bars anymore, my stomach just can't handle the glaze at my age. But anyway, Dave never needed to audit my tickets. Now, suddenly, every single thing I touch is being tracked for this supposed \"policy.\"\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing any sounds or voices that other people seem unable to hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, nothing like that. My hearing is perfectly fine. Well, mostly fine, I guess. I did have to get it checked a few years ago because I kept missing the microwave beep at home. Turns out it was just a buildup of wax. The clinic doctor used this little water spray thing to flush it out—it was freezing cold, honestly, you'd think they could warm the water up first. \n\nIt actually reminded me of the time I had to deal with a leak in the server room at my old job back in '04. The AC unit was dripping right onto the main switch, just this steady drip, drip, drip. Took the facilities guys three days to get out there, and I had to rig up a plastic tarp over the whole rack just to keep the network online. They never even thanked me for that, if you can believe it. But no, no voices or secret sounds or anything. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these specific actions by your current management are part of a targeted, organized effort to sabotage your career, or do you feel that most people in your life—including friends and family—are generally untrustworthy and likely to take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s definitely the management at work. My family, they're fine. My sister calls every Sunday, though honestly, she talks my ear off about her golden retriever, Buster. Buster this, Buster that. He chewed up her good rug last week, the Persian one she got at an estate sale. I told her she should have crate-trained him when he was a puppy, but she never listens. She’s always been like that, even when we were kids. \n\nBut at work? Oh, absolutely. It's a coordinated effort. It’s not just one person, it’s the whole chain of command. The HR director, the IT manager... they're all in on it. They know I’m close to retirement, and they want to push me out before my pension fully vests. It’s exactly what happened to Frank over in accounting back in 2012. They started nitpicking his expense reports, making him justify every single paperclip. Frank used to buy those fancy colored paperclips, the plastic-coated ones? I never saw the point of those, they slide right off the paper if you stack more than five sheets. But yeah, they drove Frank right out the door, and now they’re doing the exact same thing to me with these ticket audits. It’s a completely targeted campaign.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people at your work have access to special technology, such as reading your thoughts or monitoring you through devices in your home, to keep track of your activities?\n\nPatient: Read my thoughts? No, no, they don't have anything like that. They don't need mind-reading technology, they just use the server logs and the ticket system. It's all right there on the network. They monitor my work computer, sure, but everyone knows they do that. It's in the employee handbook. I remember when they first updated the handbook back in... oh, it must have been 2008. They handed out these thick binders to everyone. Navy blue with the company logo stamped in silver on the front. \n\nI used that binder to prop up my monitor for years. You know how those old Dell monitors were, the stands were never quite the right height for my neck. I ended up having to go to a chiropractor for a while because of it. Dr. Evans, his name was. Nice guy, always had jazz playing in the waiting room. I don't really care for jazz, it's too unpredictable. I prefer something with a steady beat, like classic rock. Anyway, no, they aren't reading my mind or putting cameras in my house. They're just using the standard IT audits to build their case against me at the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that ordinary events, like a news report, a song on the radio, or strangers talking in public, are specifically intended to send you a secret message or are referring to you personally?\n\nPatient: Secret messages? On the radio? No, definitely not. I mean, the news is just the news, right? Mostly depressing these days, to be honest. I stopped watching the evening broadcast after they changed the anchor. The old guy, Tom, he had this really reassuring voice. Reminded me of my high school shop teacher, Mr. Henderson. \n\nMr. Henderson taught me how to use a lathe. We made these wooden bowls, and I gave mine to my mother for Mother's Day back in '81. She kept potpourri in it on the coffee table for years. It was that really strong cinnamon scent—almost gave you a headache if you sat too close to it. I always preferred pine, myself. Like those little tree air fresheners you hang in the car. I had one in my first car, a '78 Honda Civic. Great gas mileage on that thing, but the heater was absolute garbage in the winter. I had to scrape the ice off the *inside* of the windshield a few times. \n\nBut no, I don't think strangers or the radio are trying to send me messages. The only people out to get me are the ones at the office, using the ticket system. That's all I'm worried about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever asked your manager or HR directly about these audits, and if so, how did you interpret their explanation that it is just company policy?\n\nPatient: Oh, I’ve asked them. I mean, that's really why I'm here, because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone—anyone—to take this seriously. I marched right into the HR office. They moved HR to the third floor last spring and gave them this \"open concept\" layout. I absolutely hate it. You can hear everyone chewing their lunch. Sandra in payroll eats baby carrots at 11 AM every single day, and the crunching just echoes off the glass walls. I really don't know why they got rid of the old cubicles. The gray fabric walls absorbed the sound so much better. Plus, you could pin things to them. I used to have this Dilbert calendar pinned up, the one where you tear off a page every day? My daughter got it for me for Christmas. \n\nAnyway, yes, I confronted management about the new ticket rotation and these security audits. I told them point-blank that I know they're using them to build a paper trail against me. And you know what they do? They just pull up these logs on their screens, or they print them out to show me. And they print them on that cheap recycled paper that constantly jams the Lexmark printers on floor two. I’m always the one who has to go unjam those things. The rubber rollers get coated in paper dust because purchasing buys the bargain reams to save a few pennies. You really have to use a lint-free cloth with a little isopropyl alcohol to clean those rollers properly. If you just blow on it with canned air, it doesn't do any good, it just moves the dust around. \n\nBut yeah, they sit there, showing me these printed logs, and they just keep repeating the exact same corporate line: \"It's just company policy, we do this for everyone.\" They think I'm stupid. But I know it's not just policy. They're just using that as an excuse while they build their paper trail to get rid of me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the stress you feel regarding your job, have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently down or, conversely, having periods where you feel unusually energetic or irritable?\n\nPatient: Mood changes? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, obviously I'm stressed about the situation at work, who wouldn't be? But I'm not depressed or bouncing off the walls or anything. I just want to do my job. It's like when I used to play softball in the company league back in the late nineties. We had this pitcher, Dave, who worked in accounting. Dave was always so high-strung, you know? If the umpire made a call he didn't like, he'd throw his glove in the dirt. Real dramatic type. \n\nI never understood getting that worked up over a game. I played right field, mostly. It's peaceful out there. You get a lot of time to just watch the grass grow. The city maintained the fields, and they always cut the grass way too short in August, so it would burn up and get all yellow and crunchy. You had to wear decent cleats or you'd slip right on the dead grass. I had a pair of Nike cleats, black with a white swoosh. Bought them at a sporting goods store that used to be over by the old mall. They tore that mall down a few years ago to build luxury condos. They look completely out of place, all glass and steel right in the middle of a brick neighborhood. \n\nBut anyway, no, my mood is fine. I'm just tired of the constant auditing and the paper trail they're making. That's the only thing bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about these audits caused you to miss work, withdraw from social activities, or change your daily routine in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't missed any work. I mean, you can't miss work when they're watching your every move, right? But I have had to change how I do things. Like, I started avoiding certain network projects completely because I know they're just setting traps in the configurations. And I spend half my day writing these massive, detailed notes in every single ticket just to cover myself. It's exhausting. \n\nI used to use this really nice mechanical keyboard for typing all those notes—a Cherry MX Brown, tactile but not too clicky, you know? The IT guys used to let us bring our own peripherals before they locked down the USB ports. Now we're stuck with these mushy membrane keyboards that Dell ships for free with the workstations. They feel like typing on wet oatmeal. My wrists ache by the end of the day. I tried bringing in one of those ergonomic gel wrist rests, the ones that look like a blue sausage, but someone stole it off my desk over the weekend. Probably the night cleaning crew. They always leave the trash cans slightly out of place, too. It drives me crazy. I have to push mine exactly two inches from the desk leg every morning. \n\nBut yeah, because I have to write all these defensive notes, it's really slowed down my work, and the team meetings have gotten incredibly tense. Nobody wants to collaborate anymore because I keep pointing out the flaws in their ticket routing. So I guess you could say my routine has changed, but only because I have to protect myself from the audits.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped seeing friends or family, or stopped participating in hobbies you used to enjoy, specifically because you are concerned that your coworkers might be tracking your activities through those interactions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think they're tracking my family. Outside of this whole mess with the ticket rotation and the audits at work, everything else is fine. I still keep my house in order, you know? I pay the bills on time, I help my wife out with whatever she needs. \n\nActually, I still write a physical check for our water bill because the city's online portal charges a $2.50 convenience fee. It's ridiculous. Two dollars and fifty cents just to process an automated clearing house transfer. It's a complete racket. The city council voted on that last November, and my neighbor, Tom, he actually went down to the town hall meeting to complain about it. Tom’s got this old basset hound that barks at the mail carrier every single day at exactly 1:15 PM. You can practically set your watch by that dog. The mail carrier drives one of those old right-hand drive Grumman LLVs, you know, the aluminum ones? They're notorious for catching fire because the windshield wiper fluid line can leak right onto the fuse box. I read a whole article about it in a mechanics magazine a few years ago. They were supposed to replace that entire fleet by 2018, but you know how government contracts work. Total bureaucratic nightmare. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my family. No, I still see my friends and family. My personal life hasn't changed. I do my chores, and honestly, I still handle all the technical parts of my job perfectly well, too. Nobody is going around saying my actual work is sloppy or disorganized. I know my stuff. Unlike the new guy they hired in Tier 1 support, Kevin. He leaves ethernet cables tangled up like a bowl of spaghetti behind the server racks. I had to spend an hour last Tuesday just untangling a Cat6 run because he didn't bother to use the velcro ties. \n\nSo, no, I haven't stopped my hobbies or anything. It's just the audits at work that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever asked a trusted coworker, someone not involved in management, if they have noticed these same security audits and ticket patterns being used against them?\n\nPatient: Ask a coworker? No, I haven't really asked anyone else about it. I mean, who can you trust, right? Even the guys who aren't in management, they're all just trying to keep their heads down and get through the day. It's like that time I tried to organize a carpool back in 2005. Gas prices were spiking—I think it was right after Hurricane Katrina hit the refineries down in the Gulf. We were paying something like three dollars a gallon, which seemed insane back then. I drove a Honda Accord, the '98 model with the V6 engine. Great car, incredibly reliable, but the transmission on those things was a known issue. You had to make sure you changed the fluid every 30,000 miles or the clutch packs would wear out prematurely. I always used the genuine Honda ATF-Z1 fluid. You could buy it by the case online for a decent discount. \n\nAnyway, I asked three guys on my shift if they wanted to split the driving, and every single one of them had some excuse. \"Oh, I have to pick up my kids,\" or \"I go to the gym after work.\" Nobody wants to stick their neck out or commit to anything. People are just inherently selfish, you know? They want the convenience without the responsibility. Like how people just leave their grocery carts in the middle of the parking lot instead of walking ten feet to the corral. The wind catches them and they slam right into your door. I got a nasty dent in my quarter panel last year at the Safeway because of that. The paint actually chipped down to the primer. \n\nSo, no, I haven't brought up the audits with anyone else. I just keep it to myself and try to stay one step ahead of the tickets.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretation of these work audits might be influenced by a health condition, rather than being an objective reality?\n\nPatient: A health condition? Like I'm making it up? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, I'm here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that this new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me these logs, pointing to the screen and saying, \"Look, it’s just policy, everyone goes through it.\" But it's not just policy. \n\nIt reminds me of when they changed the policy on the break room refrigerator back in '98. They put up this laminated sign saying anything left on Friday at 5 PM would be thrown out. \"Just policy,\" they said. I used to bring in these amazing homemade pastrami sandwiches. My wife would get the meat from this little deli downtown, Shapiro's. They slow-roasted it, sliced it paper-thin. You can't find pastrami like that anymore. Now it's all pre-packaged, water-injected garbage at the supermarket. The sodium content alone is enough to give you a stroke. I read a study once about how nitrates in deli meat are linked to all sorts of digestive issues. \n\nAnyway, I left half a sandwich in there one Friday because I got pulled into a massive server crash right at 4:45. By the time I got back on Monday, the sandwich was gone, but my Tupperware container was sitting clean on the drying rack. If they threw it out, why did they wash the container? Someone ate it, I know they did. Probably Dave from accounting. He always lingered around the fridge. \n\nSo when management tells me these audits are just policy, I know better. I know they're targeting me, trying to build a case to push me out. It has nothing to do with my health or my mind playing tricks on me. The proof is right there in the ticket assignments, even if they won't admit it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, has your concern about these audits made it difficult for you to keep up with your personal hygiene, your household chores, or your ability to get to work on time?\n\nPatient: I feel like we already covered some of this, honestly. Like I said a few minutes ago, outside of this whole issue with the ticket rotation and the audits, everything else is completely fine. I still keep my house in order, I pay the bills, I help my wife out. \n\nSpeaking of keeping the house in order, I actually just had to rebuild the brush roller on our vacuum cleaner this past weekend. It's an old Hoover WindTunnel. They don't make them like that anymore, you know. The new ones, they use these cheap plastic impellers that snap if you accidentally suck up a single penny. The older models have metal impellers. Built like a tank. Anyway, the drive belt snapped. You can't just buy them at the local hardware store anymore, you have to order them online. I had to wait four days for a two-dollar rubber belt to ship all the way from Ohio. Ohio! Why is a warehouse in Ohio handling distribution for a single residential address when there's a major fulfillment center two towns over? It makes absolutely no sense. The logistics algorithms these shipping companies use are completely backwards. They route packages through three different regional hubs just to save a fraction of a cent on bulk freight, but it ends up taking twice as long for the consumer. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, hygiene and getting to work. No, no problems there. I'm up at 5:30 every morning. Shower, shave. I actually use an old-school double-edge safety razor. Those five-blade cartridge things you see at the drugstore are a total scam. They just keep adding blades to justify charging twenty bucks a pack. It's basic marketing psychology. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm at my desk at 7:45 AM sharp every single day. I handle all the technical parts of my job perfectly. Nobody is going around saying my work is sloppy or disorganized, because it isn't. I do my job well. It's just those audits that are the problem, nothing else.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you have any special talents, insights, or a higher status that makes you a person of particular interest to your management beyond just your role as an employee?\n\nPatient: Special talents? No, I'm just a computer support specialist. I don't have any kind of secret clearance or some grand insight that nobody else has. I mean, I'm good at my job, sure. I know my way around a network topology better than most of the kids they hire straight out of college these days. They come in with their degrees and their certifications, but they don't know how to troubleshoot a legacy system. \n\nIt's like when they rolled out Windows Vista. What an absolute disaster that was. The User Account Control feature was so aggressive, it popped up a warning dialogue if you so much as breathed on the mouse. We spent six months just trying to get our proprietary inventory software to communicate with the new OS architecture. The software was written in Delphi, if you can believe it. Delphi! Nobody uses that anymore. It was developed by Borland back in the mid-90s. Actually, Borland had a really interesting corporate history. They were huge competitors with Microsoft for a while, especially with their Turbo Pascal compiler. Anders Hejlsberg, the guy who designed it, eventually got poached by Microsoft and ended up creating C#. The tech industry is incredibly incestuous like that. Everyone just moves from one giant corporation to another, taking the trade secrets with them. \n\nBut no, I'm not some special genius or anything. I'm just a guy trying to do his job while management uses these audits to build a case against me. I don't have any delusions of grandeur, if that's what you're asking. I'm just a regular employee.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's just the audits at work that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever asked a trusted coworker, someone not involved in management, if they have noticed these same security audits and ticket patterns being used against them?\n\nPatient: Ask a coworker? No, I haven't really asked anyone else about it. I mean, who can you trust, right? Even the guys who aren't in management, they're all just trying to keep their heads down and get through the day. It's like that time I tried to organize a carpool back in 2005. Gas prices were spiking—I think it was right after Hurricane Katrina hit the refineries down in the Gulf. We were paying something like three dollars a gallon, which seemed insane back then. I drove a Honda Accord, the '98 model with the V6 engine. Great car, incredibly reliable, but the transmission on those things was a known issue. You had to make sure you changed the fluid every 30,000 miles or the clutch packs would wear out prematurely. I always used the genuine Honda ATF-Z1 fluid. You could buy it by the case online for a decent discount. \n\nAnyway, I asked three guys on my shift if they wanted to split the driving, and every single one of them had some excuse. \"Oh, I have to pick up my kids,\" or \"I go to the gym after work.\" Nobody wants to stick their neck out or commit to anything. People are just inherently selfish, you know? They want the convenience without the responsibility. Like how people just leave their grocery carts in the middle of the parking lot instead of walking ten feet to the corral. The wind catches them and they slam right into your door. I got a nasty dent in my quarter panel last year at the Safeway because of that. The paint actually chipped down to the primer. \n\nSo, no, I haven't brought up the audits with anyone else. I just keep it to myself and try to stay one step ahead of the tickets.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretation of these work audits might be influenced by a health condition, rather than being an objective reality?\n\nPatient: A health condition? Like I'm making it up? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, I'm here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that this new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me these logs, pointing to the screen and saying, \"Look, it’s just policy, everyone goes through it.\" But it's not just policy. \n\nIt reminds me of when they changed the policy on the break room refrigerator back in '98. They put up this laminated sign saying anything left on Friday at 5 PM would be thrown out. \"Just policy,\" they said. I used to bring in these amazing homemade pastrami sandwiches. My wife would get the meat from this little deli downtown, Shapiro's. They slow-roasted it, sliced it paper-thin. You can't find pastrami like that anymore. Now it's all pre-packaged, water-injected garbage at the supermarket. The sodium content alone is enough to give you a stroke. I read a study once about how nitrates in deli meat are linked to all sorts of digestive issues. \n\nAnyway, I left half a sandwich in there one Friday because I got pulled into a massive server crash right at 4:45. By the time I got back on Monday, the sandwich was gone, but my Tupperware container was sitting clean on the drying rack. If they threw it out, why did they wash the container? Someone ate it, I know they did. Probably Dave from accounting. He always lingered around the fridge. \n\nSo when management tells me these audits are just policy, I know better. I know they're targeting me, trying to build a case to push me out. It has nothing to do with my health or my mind playing tricks on me. The proof is right there in the ticket assignments, even if they won't admit it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, has your concern about these audits made it difficult for you to keep up with your personal hygiene, your household chores, or your ability to get to work on time?\n\nPatient: I feel like we already covered some of this, honestly. Like I said a few minutes ago, outside of this whole issue with the ticket rotation and the audits, everything else is completely fine. I still keep my house in order, I pay the bills, I help my wife out. \n\nSpeaking of keeping the house in order, I actually just had to rebuild the brush roller on our vacuum cleaner this past weekend. It's an old Hoover WindTunnel. They don't make them like that anymore, you know. The new ones, they use these cheap plastic impellers that snap if you accidentally suck up a single penny. The older models have metal impellers. Built like a tank. Anyway, the drive belt snapped. You can't just buy them at the local hardware store anymore, you have to order them online. I had to wait four days for a two-dollar rubber belt to ship all the way from Ohio. Ohio! Why is a warehouse in Ohio handling distribution for a single residential address when there's a major fulfillment center two towns over? It makes absolutely no sense. The logistics algorithms these shipping companies use are completely backwards. They route packages through three different regional hubs just to save a fraction of a cent on bulk freight, but it ends up taking twice as long for the consumer. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, hygiene and getting to work. No, no problems there. I'm up at 5:30 every morning. Shower, shave. I actually use an old-school double-edge safety razor. Those five-blade cartridge things you see at the drugstore are a total scam. They just keep adding blades to justify charging twenty bucks a pack. It's basic marketing psychology. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm at my desk at 7:45 AM sharp every single day. I handle all the technical parts of my job perfectly. Nobody is going around saying my work is sloppy or disorganized, because it isn't. I do my job well. It's just those audits that are the problem, nothing else.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you have any special talents, insights, or a higher status that makes you a person of particular interest to your management beyond just your role as an employee?\n\nPatient: Special talents? No, I'm just a computer support specialist. I don't have any kind of secret clearance or some grand insight that nobody else has. I mean, I'm good at my job, sure. I know my way around a network topology better than most of the kids they hire straight out of college these days. They come in with their degrees and their certifications, but they don't know how to troubleshoot a legacy system. \n\nIt's like when they rolled out Windows Vista. What an absolute disaster that was. The User Account Control feature was so aggressive, it popped up a warning dialogue if you so much as breathed on the mouse. We spent six months just trying to get our proprietary inventory software to communicate with the new OS architecture. The software was written in Delphi, if you can believe it. Delphi! Nobody uses that anymore. It was developed by Borland back in the mid-90s. Actually, Borland had a really interesting corporate history. They were huge competitors with Microsoft for a while, especially with their Turbo Pascal compiler. Anders Hejlsberg, the guy who designed it, eventually got poached by Microsoft and ended up creating C#. The tech industry is incredibly incestuous like that. Everyone just moves from one giant corporation to another, taking the trade secrets with them. \n\nBut no, I'm not some special genius or anything. I'm just a guy trying to do his job while management uses these audits to build a case against me. I don't have any delusions of grandeur, if that's what you're asking. I'm just a regular employee."
  }
